Variational quantum algorithm for measurement extraction from the Navier-Stokes, Einstein, Maxwell, Boussniesq-type, Lin-Tsien, Camassa-Holm, Drinfeld-Sokolov-Wilson, Hunter-Saxton, KdV-Burgers, non-homogeneous KdV, generalized KdV, KdV, translational KdV, super KdV, Benney-Luke, and Airy equations equations
Pete Rigas

Abstract
Classical-quantum hybrid algorithms have recently garnered significant attention, which are characterized by combining quantum and classical computing protocols to obtain readout from quantum circuits of interest. Recent progress due to Lubasch et al in a 2019 paper provides readout for solutions to the Schrodinger and Inviscid Burgers equations, by making use of a new variational quantum algorithm (VQA) which determines the ground state of a cost function expressed with a superposition of expectation values and variational parameters. In the following, we analyze additional computational prospects in which the VQA can reliably produce solutions to other PDEs that are comparable to solutions that have been previously realized classically, which are characterized with noiseless quantum simulations. To determine the range of nonlinearities that the algorithm can process for other IVPs, we study several PDEs, first beginning with the Navier-Stokes equations and progressing to other equations underlying physical phenomena ranging from electromagnetism, gravitation, and wave propagation, from simulations of the Einstein, Boussniesq-type, Lin-Tsien, Camassa-Holm, Drinfeld-Sokolov-Wilson (DSW), and Hunter-Saxton equations. To formulate optimization routines that the VQA undergoes for numerical approximations of solutions that are obtained as readout from quantum circuits, cost functions corresponding to each PDE are provided in the supplementary section after which simulations results from hundreds of ZGR-QFT ansatzae are generated. With such an ensemble of ansatzae that we perform numerical experiments upon through time-evolution of quantum states corresponding to solution approximations of a PDE, we can readily compare different initial states of the solution across different PDEs that the VQA can effectively approximate, and establish rough comparisons between computational complexity of optimizing different cost functions. To quantify VQA performance for approximating solutions, we automate evaluation of our quantum circuits with the open source Cirq and QSimCirq platforms, which respectively prepare, and update, the state vector corresponding to a PDE solution state throughout the number of time steps in the evolution. To determine whether barren plateaus when optimizing for the ground state can be avoided, we also execute gradient-based, stochastic, and constrained, optimization procedures, which are compared with the performance of deterministic optimizers on a case-by-case basis. 1 1 1 Keywords : Quantum computation, variational quantum algorithms, variational quantum computing, quantum simulation, hybrid quantum algorithms, parameter shift rule, quantum embedding, gradient-based optimization, Nevergrad optimizer, Neighborhood algorithm, Particle-swarm, Competitive Particle-swarm, sequential least squares, Scipy optimizer, QSimCirq simulator, solution state vector, ansatz, measurement extraction, quantum circuit readout, time evolution, Camassa-Holm equation, generalized Camassa-Holm equation, symmetry, wave solution, soliton, standing wave, wave amplitude, time-evolution, differential polynomial ring, gradation 2 2 2 MSC Class : 81P10; 81Q04; 81V08



1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

The variational quantum algorithm (VQA) has attracted wide attention from several computational disciplines in physics, chemistry and materials science
[12]
, with recent studies demonstrating how optimizers for such algorithms can be improved and parametrized for entanglement
[17,18]
, robust encoding in molecules for chemistry computations
[1]
, various implementations for linear algebra problems
[16,20]
, variance minimization
[27]
, and the construction of quantum Gibbs states
[3]
. To further contribute to rapid developments, Lubasch et al in
[11]
introduce a new VQA which can be applied to solve nonlinear problems arising from various PDEs through specifications on quantum circuits, including an optimized quantum nonlinear processing unit (QNPU) for treating nonlinearities of the equation that is being solved for efficiently, in addition to randomly chosen variational parameters
\lambda,\lambda_{0}
which parametrize a cost function
\mathcal{C}
that is classically minimized. In turn, quantum registers prepared in circuits of polynomial depth not only require exponentially fewer parameters, but also provide ripe opportunity for the algorithm to be applied to several other nonlinear problems, some of which are presented in this article, including computational fluid dynamics problems raised by solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations, approximation of the gravitational field on different forms of matter raised by the stress-energy tensor of the Einstein field equations, distribution of electric and magnetic field interactions raised by the Maxwell equations, and wave interference, to provide a few examples.

To briefly expound upon the setting of
[11]
, a variational quantum algorithm is applied to NLS and to the inviscid Burger’s equation, with MPS ansatzae of polynomial bond dimension
[13,14]
. Such ansatzae are advantageous for quantum computation in their ability to be represented as a product of isometric matrices which can be physically interpreted as the wave function of possible states for the solution to a PDE, after which variational states for the solution are time evolved. In comparison to cost functions obtained for NLS and the Burger’s equations, the Navier-Stokes cost function, and cost functions for other PDEs provided in the Supplementary section of this paper, involve a more complicated superposition of quantum states; for example, the Navier-Stokes cost function also includes parameters that are directly proportional to the time step and inversely proportional to the Reynolds number, in addition to several other expectation terms resulting from quantum states that are acted upon by differential operators which are represented with Adder and Adder dagger operators, while the cost function for the Einstein equations involves expectation terms dependent upon the metric and stress energy tensors.

For each PDE, we derive the cost function, as a result obtaining a superposition of quantum states parameterized in nonlinearities of the equation. To implement outstanding steps of the VQA, classical, stochastic, gradient-based, and constrained, optimization routines are implemented in order to determine circuit arrangements for which the VQA is capable of generating solutions to nonlinear problems for different classes of classically prepared initial data with the ZGR-QFT ansatz. Modifications to the general requirements imposed on quantum circuits in
[11]
are provided, following the derivation and presentation of all simulation results in the Supplementary Section, in which quantum circuits for the simulating the time evolution of PDEs considered in this article are achieved with QNPUs composed of a significantly higher number of input and output ports.

To apply this hybrid algorithm, and others, for studying nonlinearities and related behaviors of nonlinear problems, it is necessary to construct quantum circuits for approximating nonlinearities with expectation values, in addition to variational parameters that are modified throughout the optimization procedure as the algorithm is executed to obtain approximations of analytical solutions to a PDE of interest. For initial exploration of the Hilbert space before the VQA further time evolves the quantum state corresponding to the PDE solution, ansatzae, including the shallow layered ansatz obtained through a series of several rotation, and entanglement, operations, as well as computation of Fourier coefficients, provide additional performance metrics.

For running the algorithm with nonlinearities besides those which are discussed in
[11]
, it is important to not only determine optimal circuit constructions that reduce noise in unitary gate operations for potential future applications of the VQA
[10,15,23]
, but also the range of time steps that the optimizer must take to arrive to a ground state in order for approximations of the solution to be convergent. Specifically, in one case, nonlinearities that the VQA processes for obtaining solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations are simulated with quantum circuits with readout values from expectations taken between operators denoted with
\hat{Q}
, in addition to the pressure
\rho
exerted on direction of fluid flow in the system. To this end, quantum circuits for simulating time evolution of Navier-Stokes solutions are of shallow depth, permitting for readout from quantum circuits for nonlinearities of the Navier-Stokes cost function with the open-source Python Cirq package, as with the other
7
PDEs that are examined in the Supplementary Section. Notably, the primary difficulties in applying the hybrid variational quantum algorithm to the Navier-Stokes equations, versus other PDEs of interest, is apparent through the QNPU construction for expectation terms from each cost function, which widely vary depending upon the order of spatial derivatives that are taken of the solution appearing in the equation, the initial conditions of the ansatz from which the optimization routine begins, the number of time steps, optimizer, amongst several other factors.

1.2
Paper organization

We present results from quantum circuits to compute all expectation values for each PDE. Beyond this starting point, within the framework of
[11]
, we formulate applications in different nonlinear problems. The quantum circuits provided in the last Supplementary section demonstrate the sequence of unitary operations from which the quantum-classical computational loop is executed, in which individual plots for approximations of PDE solutions exhibit, generally, strong agreement with solutions that can be classically predicted, either from analytically known exact solutions, or from solution approximations provided from classical numerical schemes, that have been previously realized in the literature from the list of references provided both for the Introduction and Supplementary sections. From well-established representations of quantum networks as sequences of unitary transformations that are applied to qubits initialized in the
0
state at the beginning of time evolution
[19]
, we obtain quantum readout through adaptations of the VQA of
[11]
with ZGR-QFT anstzae that are clasically simulable in polynomial time. From quantum circuit representations of expectation values expressed as a superposition from each cost function, the quantum operators corresponding to the first and second derivatives require that we introduce circuit representations for Adder, and Adder dagger, operators for processing quantum computations for time evolution.

From the QNPUs provided in quantum circuits used in
[11]
for solutions to two PDEs, the total number of qubits that are to be time evolved in a quantum circuit is indicative of the total runtime of each time evolution step for optimizing to the ground state of the cost function. To account for complexity posed by the Navier-Stokes cost function, and cost functions for other PDEs, in addition to several other computationally related challenges, ranging from those associated with vanishing gradients of the cost function to ansatz construction
[11]
, expressibility of parametrizations for quantum circuits
[17]
, qubit quality and stable quantum representations of solutions to PDE IVPs, data compression in machine learning for classification tasks, error mitigation, error correcting codes, and noise and complexity growth
[2,6]
, different forms of ansatzae for the equations will be studied and qualitatively compared. To systematically characterize the coverage of the Hilbert space that we expect from such quantum simulations, we sample entries from a state wavefunction, from which comparisons with classical solution approximations can be established.

To ensure that the VQA robustly performs across different parameters that can be continuously adjusted for in the quantum circuit model, we generate a broad ensemble of quantum states for direct comparison with classically known exact solutions to each PDE, some of which are provided at the beginning of the Supplementary Section before the derivation of each cost function, and corresponding simulation results.

3
Supplemental materials

3.1
Earlier studies of exact solutions to the Navier-Stokes, Einstein, Maxwell, Lin-Tsien, and other PDEs

Previous works have investigated several different aspects of the Navier-Stokes equations, whether it be the domain over which solutions are obtained, specific assumptions of the behavior of the pressure gradient under translations and rotations and over discretizations of varying composition
[21]
, each of which can impact the velocity profile that classically realized algorithms have predicted arise in time-dependent trajectories of fluids in two, and three, dimensional space. In higher dimensional cases beyond those considered in the two-dimensional plane, past works have studied the regularity structure of Navier-Stokes solutions, in addition to a stochastic formulation in which solutions for the Navier-Stokes equations driven by space time white noise were provided, through construction of Gaussian invariant measures and fixed point mappings
[13,21]
.

On the other hand, efforts have also been made to prove analytical solutions for typically found pressure distributions to the equations as given in
[16]
, in which usual procedures transform the PDE system to an ODE system from which standard separation of variables techniques yield solutions under specific circumstances and initial conditions. Under the expected initial conditions, solutions for the velocity components of Navier-Stokes in two or three dimensions exponentially decay with respect to time, in one case. From characteristics of such solutions and other closely related aspects of the Navier-Stokes equations, authors in previous works have observed that conservation laws of the system for other quantities, whether it be helicity,
2
D entrophy or total vorticity of the trajectory, allow for numerical methods that are capable of simulating the pressure distribution across three dimensional objects including a square cylinder
[9]
, sharing connections to desirable quantities which can be physically interpreted as the drag and drift forces on an airplane wing throughout a flight. For computation of aerodynamic pressure exerted upon an airplane wing, velocity profiles of fluids and gases must be approximated from components of the Navier-Stokes equations, which can yield a reconstruction of a two or three-dimensional vector field. Relatedly, the authors of
[11]
provide formulations of the Navier-Stokes and Stokes equations for incompressible fluid flow, while the authors of
[2]
avoid finite differencing of the equations altogether, still obtaining solutions, however, with unknown pressure as initial conditions, in directly opposition to the assumptions imposed on solutions to the Navier-Stokes equations obtained from the procedure in
[16]
.

For other nonlinear problems, observations stemming primarily from Riemannian geometry and connections to Ricci curvature inform the structure of the stress-energy tensor for several different numerical studies of the Einstein equation, in which the authors of
[3]
establish variational principles allowing for arguments involving the covariant derivative, as symmetry of the Ricci curvature tensor with a damped Laplacian is formalized. Plots in the upcoming subsections of this Supplementary Section exhibit plots of solutions to Navier-Stokes for the
x
and
y
components of the velocity solution, and furthermore, comparisons of the performance of different ZGR-QFT anstzae in the coverage of the Hilbert space. For remaining PDEs of interest, references for exact solutions similarly exist, allowing for comparisons of the performance of the VQA, upon comparing to exact solutions
[1
,
5
,
6
,
9]
.

3.2
Quantum circuit architecture

Before executing gates of the QNPU, to trace output of control qubits in the circuit we illustrate the number of initialized
\ket{0}
states required for the execution of the variational algorithm from
[13]
, in which qubits before being time evolved to the QNPU must be appropriately manipulated. For nonlinearities of the Navier-Stokes equations, executing nonlinearities raised by the cost function for the QNPU alone requires that we include additional qubits so that all expectation values from the superposition in the cost function can be added, and multiplied, together with a sufficient number of ancilla qubits. Moreover, we appropriately situate the input ports into the QNPU from previous gate operations in the circuit following the
\ket{0}
state initialization, allowing us to perform circuit simulations on a variable number of target qubits that are specifically determined for each PDE.

The Navier-Stokes QNPU processes target qubits following previous gate operations, which contain an Adder, for computing the resulting quantum state which approximates the quantum ground state following the time step magnitude of the evolution. To accommodate the circuit construction so that all gates reflect the capability of the algorithm to be applied to other nonlinear equations, we incorporate standard variational parameters
\lambda
over which the cost function is optimized. For different cost functions, the computational cost of operations associated with approximating
C(\lambda,\lambda_{0})
include scalar multiplication involving the variational parameter
\lambda_{0}
with itself, multiplication for various expectation values that are dependent on the pressure, and spatial geometry of the lattice over which the solution is approximated, with the scalar variational parameter product
\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\text{ }\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}
, and
\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}
, as appearing in the Navier-Stokes cost function. Other fundamental constants, whether Newton’s gravitation constant or the permitivity of free space, parametrize the quantum circuit and QNPU of other PDEs, which respectively appear in cost functions provdied for the Einstein and Maxwell equations. Besides computing such expectation value, additional computations must be dealt with by isolating all accompanying prefactors for each expectation value, including
-\frac{\tau}{h}\lambda_{0}^{*}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}
,
\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\lambda_{0}^{*}
, and
-\frac{1}{\rho}\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}
, respectively, which is achieved by forming a quantum superposition for the cost function before different optimizers, time step magnitude, initial conditions, and ansatzae are implemented. Finally, when forming the quantum superposition encoding the ground state, additional ancilla for the control ports (CPs) to the QNPU are immediately accounted for by Cirq when the resultant state is approximated when sampling from the solution state vector prepared with QSimCirq. In the following circuit diagrams provided in the last section of the Supplementary materials, we establish all such specifications on the ancilla within the computational basis, and provide similarly related quantum circuit arragements that were implemented in the cost function optimization for other nonlinear PDEs besides the previously solved PDEs. We gather results by reading off expectation values from the each cost function, and afterwards provide quantum simulation results for approximations of several solutions.

For Navier-Stokes quantum circuits, we stipulate that the control ports preceding the QNPU are acted upon an ancilla block of polynomial depth for operations associated with adding expectation values together, which in the case for other PDEs holds for QNPUs with more IPs. With exception to the topmost control qubits which undergo a Hadamard gate before entering the QNPU through their respective IPs, additional ancilla within the QNPU are allotted for constructing the quantum superposition for the ground state at each time step of the evolution. With the quantum Fourier Transform (QFT), quantum network representations for dividing scalars can be achieved by loading the divisor and dividend into quantum registers, with the scheme being applicable to computations involving each expectation term. After each expectation is individually computed, the resulting superposition state is uniquely parametrized for each PDE. Before qubits are time evolved into the QNPU, for each cost function the ancilla block on the topmost layer, designated in
blue
, contains an arbitrary number of control qubits, each of which undergoes a Hadamard test, while additional ancilla qubits are responsible for any other computations when time evolving each target qubit represented in the quantum circuit.

Besides the
\mathrm{QNPU}
block which treats nonlinearities of each PDE from expectation terms in the cost function, the
\mathrm{H+Readout}
block applies the Hadamard gate to each ancilla after the QNPU and reads out values produced in qubits of the circuit that are time-evolved with an application of
\hat{\sigma_{z}}
. Furthermore, it is important to carefully keep track of the total number of control qubits associated with addition or multiplication on the quantum computer from the prefactors that have been previously identified. In light of rearrangements of each
\mathcal{C}
beginning for the Navier-Stokes equations in
Section 4.2
, the
\mathrm{"Clifford+T"}
gate family is applied to individually load divisors, in which components of the superposition state dividend are loaded into each CP, after which a certain number of time steps are combined with expectation values for QNPU IPs. Outside of the QNPU, results of quantum computation for the cost function are terminated for the ancilla, and other qubits of the wavefunction, are readout from the
\mathrm{H+Readout}
block.

4
Supplementary section organization

4.1
PDE library

To substantially enlarge the spectrum of nonlinearities that quantum simulators can process, we make use of the Google open source platform Cirq to simulate wavefunctions for solutions to various PDEs. From the following PDE library consisting of the following
8
PDEs, under specified initial conditions we simulate iterations of the quantum-classical hybrid algorithm for approximating previously studied classically solutions. To exhibit wide applicability of the Lubasch et al VQA, plots of solutions to each equation are provided following the cost function derivation. Quantum circuit diagrams after the cost function presented in the following sections provide an overlay of the sequence of unitary transformations which are performed on qubits in each step of time evolution before each cost function
\mathcal{C}
is optimized, in several conditions.

4.2
Cost function derivations for simulating optimization to the quantum ground state

The cost functions corresponding to each quantum circuit diagram are separately derived.
We collect all expectation values from relevant terms appearing in the statement of each PDE, and impose initial conditions on the time evolution with different ZGR-QFT ansatzae across hundreds of simulations. Initially we prepare each qubit in the
0
state and furthermore, examine the
\hat{U}_{j}(\lambda)
gates, where
j
runs over the number of qubits for each cost function
\mathcal{C}
. A subset of the expectation terms from each cost function are represented in the QNPU for measurement of components of the analytical solution, from which further discussion is devoted towards quantum circuit architecture with Figures in the last Supplementary Section.

4.3
Navier-Stokes equations

Cost function derivation

To quantify time evolution forwards at the initial state and after an increment, we introduce the states
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
. To avoid confusion with the operator
\hat{Q}
in the cost function, below, the Navier-Stokes cost function is of the form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{p,x}(\lambda,\lambda_{0})=\big(\lambda^{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}}_{0}\big)^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\big(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}}_{0}\big)^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}+\text{ }2\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{-\frac{\tau}{h}\lambda_{0}^{*}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\braket{\Psi|\hat{Q}|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\lambda_{0}\braket{\Psi|\nabla_{x}|p}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\rho}\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\braket{p|\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\hat{Q}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg\}\text{ , }

which is readily obtained upon collection of terms from the expansion of
||\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}-\frac{\tau}{h}\hat{Q}\ket{\Psi}+\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\nabla_{x}p(t)||^{2}
after rearrangement, in which we make use of the straightforward identities for discrete approximations of derivatives in the Navier-Stokes equations after finite differencing, which are,

\displaystyle\hat{Q}\ket{\Psi}=\bra{\Psi}\hat{Q}^{\dagger}\text{ }\text{ }\text{ , }

by virtue of Hermitianity being preserved. Next, the Euler method is applied to obtain
\mathcal{C}
for variational state preparation, in order to determine ground states in the optimization landscape. From the Navier-Stokes cost function, we identify variational states that are to prepared when the algorithm from
[13]
is ran. Specifically, the operator
\hat{Q}
in
\mathcal{C}
emerges from finite differencing the Navier-Stokes equations and isolating for the time increment of the state of the solution
f
forwards in time by an increment
\tau
, and takes the form, with
1
as the identity operator,

\displaystyle\hat{Q}\equiv\frac{h}{\tau}\text{ }\big(\text{ }\textbf{1}-\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}-\text{ }v\nabla_{y}+v\Delta_{x}+v\Delta_{y}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\text{ , }

where in
\hat{Q}
above,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}=\frac{1}{\Delta x}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A_{x}-\textbf{1}\text{ }\big)\text{ , }\nabla_{y}=\frac{1}{\Delta y}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A_{y}-\textbf{1}\text{ }\big)\text{ , }

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}=\frac{1}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{x}\text{ }\big)\text{ and, }\Delta_{y}=\frac{1}{(\Delta y)^{2}}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{y}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{y}\text{ }\big)\text{ . }

We will make use of this procedure to study nonlinearities of the Euler and Poisson equations, in which quantum circuit architecture for computing expectation terms of the cost function above is executed for other PDEs with modifications to the QNPU and CP. After further discussion of the circuit outputs from the variational algorithm for Navier-Stokes, such modifications to the QNPU for the Euler and Poisson equations are valuable for analyzing computational bases with respect to nonlinear behaviors of inviscid flow, amongst other types of time evolution.

From the definition of
\hat{Q}
, we observe that the second and third expectations can be rewritten as,

\displaystyle\braket{\Psi|\hat{Q}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv\braket{\Psi|\bigg(\frac{h}{\tau}\text{ }\big(\textbf{1}-\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}-\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{y}+v\text{ }\Delta_{x}+v\text{ }\Delta_{y}\big)\text{ }\bigg)|\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle\approx\braket{\Psi|\frac{h}{\tau}|\widetilde{\Psi}}-\braket{\Psi|\frac{h}{\tau}|\widetilde{\Psi}}-\braket{\Psi|\text{ }\frac{h}{\tau}v\text{ }|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|\frac{h}{\tau}\big(v\Delta_{x}\big)|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|\frac{h}{\tau}\big(v\Delta_{y}\big)|\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{\tau}\bigg(\braket{\Psi|\widetilde{\Psi}}-\braket{\Psi|\widetilde{\Psi}}-\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{x}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }+\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{y}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg)\text{ , }

which amounts to computing the summation of expectations above, with the final resultant multiplied by
\frac{h}{\tau}
corresponding to the second term. As for the third term, we can similarly expand by making use of the definition of
\hat{Q}
, hence obtaining,

\displaystyle p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\hat{Q}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv p\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\bigg(\frac{h}{\tau}(\textbf{1}-\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}-\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{y}+v\Delta_{x}+v\Delta_{y})\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=\text{ }p\text{ }\frac{h}{\tau}\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\bigg(-\text{ }p\frac{h}{\tau}\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\big(\ket{\Psi}\big)\nabla_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg)-\text{ }p\frac{h}{\tau}\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{y}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\text{ }p\text{ }\frac{hv\Delta_{x}}{\tau}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\cdots\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }p\text{ }\frac{hv\Delta_{y}}{\tau}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{h}{\tau}\bigg(\text{ }p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}-\text{ }p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\nabla_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\big)-\text{ }p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{y}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }+p\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle+p\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{y}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg)\text{ , }

allowing for further rearrangements from respective collection of like terms in
v
,
\ket{f(t)}
,
v\Delta_{x}
and
v\Delta_{y}
, between the second and third expectation terms, giving

\displaystyle\frac{h}{\tau}\bigg(-\text{ }p\text{ }v\text{ }+\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{x}\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ , }

for
\ket{v(t)}
, while,

\displaystyle-\frac{hp}{\tau}\text{ }\bigg(\ket{\Psi}+\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\big)\text{ , }

for
\ket{f(t)}
. Also, the remaining two terms dependent on
v
and
p
take the form,

\displaystyle\frac{2h}{\tau}\bigg(\text{ }p\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{x}\ket{\tilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|v\Delta_{y}|\tilde{\Psi}}\bigg)\text{ . }

Applying linearity of expectation where appropriate in turn gives a cost function of the form, from the superposition,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}+\text{ }\frac{2\text{ }h}{\tau}\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\braket{\Psi|\widetilde{\Psi}}+p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}-\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }\widetilde{\Psi}}+p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-p\text{ }\bigg(\ket{\Psi}+\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\text{ }2\bigg(p\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|v\Delta_{y}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg)\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ , }

which can be rearranged more explicitly, through substitution in terms with
\nabla_{x}
,
\nabla_{y}
,
\Delta_{x}
and
\Delta_{y}
, for the second term taken under
\mathrm{Re}
,

\displaystyle p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}=\frac{p}{\text{ }\Delta x\text{ }}\text{ }\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\big)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ , }

and similarly for the third and fourth terms taken under
\mathrm{Re}\big\{\cdot\big\}
,

\displaystyle\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }|\widetilde{\Psi}}+p\text{ }\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }v\text{ }\nabla_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg(\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\text{ }v\text{ }\{A_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ . }

For the fourth term taken under
\mathrm{Re}\big\{\cdot\big\}
, we get

\displaystyle p\text{ }\big(\ket{\Psi}+\nabla^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\Psi}\nabla_{x}\big)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg(\ket{\Psi}+\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\ket{\Psi}\{A_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ . }

Finally, from the fourth term multiplied by two,

\displaystyle p\text{ }v\text{ }\Delta_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|v\Delta_{y}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }\bigg(A^{\dagger}_{x}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{x}\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|\text{ }\frac{v}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg(A^{\dagger}_{y}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{y}\bigg)|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ , }

from which we collect all rearrangements, in turn obtaining a cost function of the form from like terms,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}+\text{ }\frac{2\text{ }h}{\tau}\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\braket{\Psi|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\text{ }\frac{p}{\Delta x}\text{ }\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\braket{\Psi|\text{ }v\text{ }\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}\text{ }}\text{ }\bigg(\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\text{ }v\text{ }\{A_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}\text{ }}\text{ }\bigg(\ket{\Psi}+\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\ket{\Psi}\{A_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }

\displaystyle+\text{ }2\bigg(\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }\bigg(A^{\dagger}_{x}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{x}\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle+\text{ }\frac{v}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\braket{\Psi|\bigg(A^{\dagger}_{y}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{y}\bigg)|\widetilde{\Psi}}\bigg)\bigg\}\text{ , }

from which rearranging fifth expectation term under
\mathrm{Re}
gives, by making use of the identity
\ket{f(t)}=\lambda_{0}\ket{\Psi}
, which is related to the time evolved quantum state which satisfies
\ket{f(t+\tau)}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}
for variational states,

\displaystyle\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{3}\text{ }}\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }\{\tilde{\lambda_{0}}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\{\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\}\{A_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\bigg)\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\equiv\bigg(\text{ }\frac{p}{\Delta x}\text{ }{\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\}\text{ }}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}

\displaystyle\times\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\}(A_{x}-\textbf{1})\bigg)\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle\equiv\text{ }\frac{p}{\Delta x}\text{ }\{\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}+\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-\textbf{1}\}\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\}}(A_{x}-\textbf{1})\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ , }

which yields the desired cost function for Navier-Stokes below after substituting in for variational states for the solution to the Navier-Stokes equations, yielding the cost function,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}(\lambda,\lambda^{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}}_{0})}\text{ }=\text{ }\big(\lambda^{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}}_{0}\big)^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\big(\tilde{\lambda}^{\mathcal{C}^{p,x}}_{0}\big)^{\text{ }2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}+\text{ }\frac{2\text{ }h}{\tau}\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\braket{\Psi|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{p}{\Delta x}\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\frac{p}{\Delta x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }

\displaystyle+\text{ }\bra{\Psi}v\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}A_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }vA^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }-\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }A_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }+\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}v\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-p\text{ }\widetilde{\lambda_{0}}\text{ }\braket{\widetilde{\Psi}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }-\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\widetilde{\lambda_{0}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\frac{2p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\widetilde{\lambda_{0}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{\Psi}}A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }

\displaystyle+\frac{p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\widetilde{\lambda_{0}}\text{ }\braket{\widetilde{\Psi}|\widetilde{\Psi}}+\text{ }\text{ }\frac{2p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\frac{4p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }v\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }+\frac{2p}{(\Delta x)^{2}}v\text{ }A_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }+\frac{v}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{\Psi}}A^{\dagger}_{y}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2v}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\braket{\widetilde{\Psi}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }+\frac{v}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bra{\Psi}A_{y}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ . }

4.3.1
Couette flow from the Navier-Stokes equations without a pressure gradient

Under a pressure distribution with no magnitude at every point on the domain over which readout for Navier-Stokes is produced, the cost function simplifies to the following. We cross off an expectation value if it is dependent on the pressure
p
, yielding the distribution,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\text{ }C,x}(\lambda,\lambda^{\mathcal{C}^{\text{ }C,x}}_{0})=\big(\lambda^{\mathcal{C}^{\text{ }C,x}}_{0}\big)^{2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{h}\big)^{2}\text{ }\tilde{\lambda}_{0}^{2}+\big(\frac{\tau}{\rho h}\big)^{2}+\text{ }\frac{2\text{ }h}{\tau}\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\bra{\Psi}\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}+\bra{\Psi}v\ket{\widetilde{\Psi}}

\displaystyle-\text{ }2\text{ }v\text{ }\braket{\Psi|A^{\dagger}_{x}-2\text{ }\textbf{1}+A_{x}|\widetilde{\Psi}}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }

for the cost function.

Figure 1:
Plots of Navier-Stokes solutions for Couette flow.
In the variational quantum simulations above, solution points to the Navier-Stokes equations in the absence of pressure gradients are colored in blue, green, or red. From the curvature of solutions curves that are optimized with Nevergran or Scipy Imfil optimizers, the Nevergran optimizer exhibits optimal performance in capturing the behavior of the quantum state encoding the Navier-Stokes solution as the solution relaxes to the steady-state velocity.

Figure 2:
Additional plots of Navier-Stokes solutions for Couette flow.
Under the Nevergran optimizer, solutions to the Navier-Stokes equation for Couette flow relax to the steady-state velocity, after which the solution continues to fluctuate. In the third plot, a direct comparison of the Navier-Stokes solutions obtained from the Imfil and Nevergran optimizers exhibits that solution approximations provided by Nevergran optimizer fluctuate significantly less often below the solution axis than solution approximations provided by the Imfil optimizer do.

Figure 3:
Plots of Navier-Stokes solutions obtained with stochastic optimizers CPSO, AND CMAES.
Under stochastic optimizers, solution approximations to Navier-Stokes Couette flow flutctuate more significantly below. andabove the solution axis, as do approximations produced by the Imfil optimizer provided in the previous figure.

Plots of Navier-Stokes sinusoidal solutions.
By comparison with exact analytical solutions obtained from Picard iterates, solution approximations obtained from readout of the variational quantum algorithm produces approximations of analytically known sinusoidal solutions.

4.4
Einstein field equations

Cost function derivation

To isolate components of the metric component necessary for the description of trajectories in spacetime, we recall that the statement of the field equations with a vanishing cosmological constant
\Delta
takes the form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}-Rg_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow\text{ }

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Gamma^{i}_{jm}}{\partial x^{k}}-\frac{\partial\Gamma^{i}_{jk}}{\partial x^{m}}+\Gamma^{i}_{rk}\Gamma^{r}_{jm}-\Gamma^{i}_{rm}\Gamma^{r}_{jk}-Rg_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}\text{ , }

where the Ricci curvature terms on the left hand side of the field equations are expressed from commutators of Christoffel symbols and their derivatives, from which further rearrangement into second order partial derivatives of the metric tensor implies,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\frac{\partial^{2}g_{jm}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{n}}-\frac{\partial^{2}g_{nj}}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{m}}-\frac{\partial^{2}g_{im}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{n}}+\frac{\partial^{2}g_{ni}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{m}}\big)-Rg_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\underbrace{\big\{\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}}_{\textbf{1}}g_{jm}-\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}}_{\textbf{2}}g_{nj}\big\}}_{\bigstar}-\underbrace{\big\{\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}}_{\textbf{3}}g_{im}-\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}}_{\textbf{4}}g_{ni}\big\}\big)}_{\bigstar\bigstar}-Rg_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}

Proceeding further, the operators designated
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, on the right hand side are respectively equivalent to the quantities
\bigstar
and
\bigstar\bigstar
below, each of which are scaled in products of differences about any degree of freedom,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\big)\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\big(\Delta_{i}\big)^{-1}\big(A_{i}-\textbf{1}\big)\big(\frac{A_{n}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{A_{m}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta_{m}}\big)

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\big(A_{i}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\big(A_{n}-\textbf{1}\big)-\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\big(A_{i}-\textbf{1}\big)\big(\text{ }A_{m}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\{A_{i}A_{n}-A_{i}-A_{n}+\textbf{1}\}}_{\textbf{1}}-\text{ }\underbrace{\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\{A_{i}A_{m}-A_{i}-A_{m}+\textbf{1}\}}_{\textbf{2}}\text{ , }

and,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\big)\equiv\big(\Delta_{j}\big)^{-1}{\big(A_{j}-\textbf{1}\big)}\big(\frac{A_{n}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{A_{m}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta_{m}}\big)\text{ }

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\text{ }\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}\big(A_{j}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\big(\text{ }A_{n}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }-\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\big(A_{j}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\big(A_{m}-\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\text{ }\underbrace{\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}\{A_{j}A_{n}-A_{j}-A_{n}+\textbf{1}\}}_{\textbf{3}}-\text{ }\underbrace{\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{m}}\{A_{j}A_{m}-A_{j}-A_{m}+\textbf{1}\}}_{\textbf{4}}\text{ . }

The resulting operator superposition of the leftmost term on the right hand side of the field equations, which is itself a superposition of two components of the metric tensor, takes the form,
3

3

With abuse of notation, set
g_{jm}\equiv\ket{g_{jm}}
for the quantum state prepared corresponding to each component of the metric tensor,and
T_{\mu\nu}\equiv\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}

,

\displaystyle\textbf{1}\ket{g_{jm}}-\textbf{2}\ket{g_{nj}}\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\big(\text{ }A_{i}A_{n}-A_{i}-A_{n}+\textbf{1}\text{ }\big)\ket{g_{jm}}-\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\big(\text{ }A_{i}A_{m}-A_{i}-A_{m}+\textbf{1}\text{ }\big)\ket{g_{nj}}

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\text{ }\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\big(A_{i}A_{n}\ket{g_{jm}}-A_{i}\ket{g_{jm}}-A_{n}\ket{g_{jm}}+\ket{g_{jm}}\big)\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\big(A_{i}A_{m}\text{ }\ket{g_{nj}}-A_{i}\ket{g_{nj}}-A_{m}\ket{g_{nj}}+\ket{g_{nj}}\text{ }\big)

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\big(\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}-\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\ket{g_{nj}}\big)-\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\big(\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{m}}\big)-\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}+\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\ket{g_{nj}}}_{\bigstar}\text{ }

\displaystyle\textbf{3}\ket{g_{im}}-\textbf{4}\ket{g_{ni}}\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A_{j}A_{n}-A_{j}-A_{n}+\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\ket{g_{im}}-\frac{1}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\big(\text{ }A_{j}A_{m}-A_{j}-A_{m}+\textbf{1}\big)\text{ }\ket{g_{ni}}\text{ }

\displaystyle+A_{j}\ket{g_{ni}}+A_{m}\ket{g_{ni}}-\ket{g_{ni}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{ni}}+\frac{\ket{g_{im}}}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{ni}}}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{m}}}_{\bigstar\bigstar}\text{ , }

which is equivalent to
\bigstar\bigstar
. Consolidating terms from each superposition of quantum states yields,

\displaystyle\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\bigg(\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ }-\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\ket{g_{nj}}\text{ }\bigg)-\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\bigg(\text{ }\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\bigg)-\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\bigg(\text{ }\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{i}}+\frac{\ket{g_{im}}}{\Delta_{j}}\bigg)+\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{i}}-\frac{\ket{g_{ni}}}{\Delta_{j}}\text{ }\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\Delta_{i}}\bigg(\text{ }\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\bigg)\text{ , }

for the first

Altogether, the terms on the right hand side of the field equations which are not first or second derivatives of components of the metric tensor either yield spatial derivatives of the time-evolved variational state for the metric tensor, or time-evolved spatial derivatives of the variational state. From the superposition, as in the derivation of the previous cost function we isolate variational states associated with metric tensor components, with the following decomposition, dependent on the order to which the metric tensor is differentiated,

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\big(\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}-\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\ket{g_{nj}}\big)+\frac{A_{j}}{\Delta_{j}}\big(\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{im}}-\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\ket{g_{ni}}\big)}_{\text{Second order terms}}-\underbrace{\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}}\big(\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{m}}\big)+\frac{A_{j}}{\Delta_{j}}\big(\frac{\ket{g_{im}}}{\Delta_{n}}\text{ }}_{\text{First order terms}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\frac{\ket{g_{ni}}}{\Delta_{m}}\big)-\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}}\big(\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{i}}+\frac{\ket{g_{im}}}{\Delta_{j}}\big)\text{ }\text{ }}_{\text{First order terms}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}}\big(\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{i}}-\frac{\ket{g_{ni}}}{\Delta_{j}}\big)}_{\text{First order terms}}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}\text{ }\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }-\big(\underbrace{\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}-\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}-\frac{\ket{g_{im}}}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{n}}+\frac{\ket{g_{ni}}}{\Delta_{j}\Delta_{m}}-R\text{ }\ket{g_{\mu\nu}}}_{\text{Zeroth order terms}}\big)\text{ . }

\displaystyle\frac{A_{i}A_{n}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}-\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\ket{g_{jm}}-\frac{A_{n}}{\Delta_{n}\Delta_{i}}\ket{g_{jm}}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}\text{ }\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{\ket{g_{jm}}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}\text{ , }

\displaystyle\mp\frac{A_{i}A_{m}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\ket{g_{nj}}\pm\frac{A_{i}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\ket{g_{nj}}\pm\frac{A_{m}}{\Delta_{m}\Delta_{i}}\text{ }\ket{g_{nj}}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}\text{ }\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }\pm\frac{\ket{g_{nj}}}{\Delta_{i}\Delta_{m}}\text{ }\text{ . }

From the relationship between the variational states, and spatial derivatives of variational states for the metric tensor, the time evolved variational state for the solution to the field equations takes the form,

\displaystyle\ket{\widetilde{g}}\text{ }=\text{ }-\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}+\ket{g_{jm}}+A_{i}\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}-A_{i}\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ , }

metric tensor component, in which
(g_{jm})_{\tau}

, and

represents the variational state before the time evolution step. The resulting quantum state whose ground state is to be determined from the cost function takes the following form after expansion,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{E}(\lambda,\lambda^{E}_{0})=\bigg|\bigg|\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}-\ket{g_{jm}}\bigg|\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\text{ }\boxed{\mathcal{C}^{E}(\lambda,\lambda^{E}_{0})}=\text{ }\big(\lambda^{E}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\bra{g_{jm}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }A_{i}\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{16\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }\text{ }

\displaystyle+\text{ }\text{ }\frac{16\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }\ket{T_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }-\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\big)^{2}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }\text{ }

in which we denote the quantum state for the metric tensor, and the time evolved state of the quantum solution representative, respectively with
(g_{jm})\equiv\ket{g_{jm}}
, and
(g_{jm})_{\tau}\equiv\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}
. As a result, the square of the norm between the time evolved variational state and the preceding state takes the form above. To numerically simulate solutions in the space-time manifold, we present several of the incarnations below for the stress energy tensor.

\bullet

Point particle test.
The expression of the stress energy tensor for the gravitational pull exerted upon a single microscopic body in space-time is,

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{m\text{ }v_{\mu}(t)\text{ }v_{\nu}(t)\text{ }}{\sqrt{1-(\frac{v}{c})^{2}}}\text{ }\bigg|x-x(t)\bigg|\text{ }\text{ , }

where
m
is the mass of the test particle traveling at velocity
v
with trajectory
x(t)
, and
v_{\mu}
and
v_{\nu}
are the velocity vectors for
\mu\text{ },\text{ }\nu\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. Under this assumption, the resulting cost function is,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}_{p}^{E}(\lambda,\lambda^{E}_{0})}=\text{ }\big(\lambda^{E}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\text{ }2\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\bra{g_{jm}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }A_{i}\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }-\text{ }2\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\frac{m\text{ }v_{\mu}(t)\text{ }v_{\nu}(t)\text{ }}{\sqrt{1-(\frac{v}{c})^{2}}}\text{ }\bigg|x-x(t)\bigg|

\displaystyle\times\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ }+\text{ }2\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\frac{m\text{ }v_{\mu}(t)\text{ }v_{\nu}(t)\text{ }}{\sqrt{1-(\frac{v}{c})^{2}}}\text{ }\bigg|x-x(t)\bigg|A_{i}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\bigg(\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bigg)^{2}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ . }

\bullet

Equilibrium fluid test.
In thermodynamic equilibrium, the stress-energy tensor is,

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}\equiv\big(\text{ }\rho+\frac{p}{c^{2}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }u_{\mu}\text{ }u_{\nu}\text{ }+\text{ }p\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{1}{g_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }\big)\text{ , }

where
\rho
is the mass-energy density,
p
the hydrostatic pressure,
u_{\mu}
and
u_{\nu}
are the four velocities of the equilibrium fluid, implying that the cost function takes the form,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}_{f}^{E}(\lambda,\lambda^{E}_{0})}\equiv\text{ }\big(\lambda^{E}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\text{ }2\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\bra{g_{jm}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }A_{i}\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }-\text{ }2\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }

\displaystyle\times\big(\text{ }\rho+\frac{p}{c^{2}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }u_{\mu}\text{ }u_{\nu}\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ }+\text{ }\text{ }p\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{1}{\ket{g_{\mu\nu}}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ }+\text{ }2\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}

\displaystyle\times\text{ }\big(\text{ }\rho+\frac{p}{c^{2}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }u_{\mu}\text{ }u_{\nu}\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}+\text{ }p\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{1}{\ket{g_{\mu\nu}}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bigg)^{2}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ . }

\bullet

Electromagnetic field tensor
.
For an electromagnetic field
\mathcal{E}
, the corresponding stress-energy tensor is,

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\text{ }\big(\text{ }F_{\mu\alpha}g^{\alpha\beta}F_{\nu\beta}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}\text{ }F^{\delta\gamma}F_{\delta\gamma}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\text{ , }

where the first multiplicative factor is the reciprocal of the permeability, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. The corresponding cost function with the electromagnetic tensor is,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}^{E}(\lambda,\lambda^{E}_{0})}\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\big(\lambda^{E}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\bra{g_{jm}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\text{ }A_{i}\ket{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}\text{ }\mp\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{2}{\mu_{0}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }F_{\mu\alpha}\ket{g_{\alpha\beta}}F_{\nu\beta}\text{ }

\displaystyle\times\text{ }A_{i}\text{ }\ket{g_{jm}}\text{ }-\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{F^{\delta\gamma}F_{\delta\gamma}\text{ }}{4\text{ }\mu_{0}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\bra{g_{jm}}A^{\dagger}_{i}\ket{g_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }\text{ }\pm\text{ }\big(\frac{2}{\mu_{0}}\big)\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{g_{jm}}}A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }F_{\mu\alpha}\text{ }\text{ }\ket{g_{\alpha\beta}}F_{\nu\beta}\text{ }

\displaystyle\text{ }-\frac{4\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }F^{\delta\gamma}F_{\delta\gamma}\text{ }\ket{g_{\mu\nu}}\text{ }-\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }\frac{8\pi G\Delta_{i}\Delta_{n}}{c^{4}}\text{ }\bigg)^{2}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ . }

4.5
Maxwell equations

Cost function derivation

The time evolved variational state for components of the eletric and magnetic fields whose oscillations and trajectories throughout space are governed by the Maxwell equations can be similarly obtained, in which for three degrees of freedom in standard Euclidean space, the equations take the form,

\displaystyle\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},\frac{\partial}{\partial y},\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\big)\times(E_{\textbf{x}},E_{\textbf{y}},E_{\textbf{z}})=-\frac{\partial(B_{\textbf{x}},B_{\textbf{y}},B_{\textbf{z}})}{\partial t}

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\textbf{x}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}E_{\textbf{z}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}E_{\textbf{y}}\big)-\textbf{y}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}E_{\textbf{z}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}E_{\textbf{x}}\big)+\textbf{z}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}E_{\textbf{y}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial y}E_{\textbf{x}}\big)=-\frac{\partial(B_{\textbf{x}},B_{\textbf{y}},B_{\textbf{z}})}{\partial t}

.

Resolving the electric and magnetic fields by each component, and subsequently substituting in for the variationally prepared states for the electric field, implies,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textbf{x}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}E_{\textbf{z}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}E_{\textbf{y}}\big)=-\frac{\partial B_{\textbf{1}}}{\partial t}\\
-\textbf{y}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}E_{\textbf{z}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}E_{\textbf{x}}\big)=-\frac{\partial B_{\textbf{y}}}{\partial t}\\
\textbf{z}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}E_{\textbf{y}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial y}E_{\textbf{x}}\big)=-\frac{\partial B_{\textbf{z}}}{\partial t}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow\text{ }

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textbf{x}\big(\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\frac{A_{z}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\big)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\big(\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{x}}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}\big)\\
-\textbf{y}\big(\frac{A_{x}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta x}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\frac{A_{z}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\big)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\big(\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\big)\\
\textbf{z}\big(\frac{A_{x}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta x}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}-\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\big)=-\frac{1}{\tau}\big(\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\big)\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow\text{ }

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\tau\big(\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\frac{A_{z}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\big)+\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}=\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{x}}}}\\
\tau\big(\frac{A_{x}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta x}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\frac{A_{z}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\big)+\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}=\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\\
-\tau\big(\frac{A_{x}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta x}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}-\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\big)+\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}=\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ . }\end{array}\right.

From variational states corresponding to the magnetic field obtained from states prepared for the electric field, substitution for components of the electric field yields the following cost function dependent on spatial derivatives of the magnetic field, whose entries read off as,

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathrm{Sgn}}_{=+1\text{ for i odd},=-1\text{ for i even }}\text{ }\tau\text{ }\big(\text{ }\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}-\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }\big)\text{ }=\frac{\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}{\tau}\big(\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{x}}}}-\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\big)\text{ . }

(
\mathrm{Sgn\text{ }equation}
)

From the corresponding variational state for the electric field, the index
i
for the electric field has corresponding indices
j
for the magnetic field which can be read off from the table below,

i
(RHS of
\mathrm{Sgn\text{ }equation}
)

y

z

A_{y}

A_{z}

\Delta y

\Delta z

1

3

2

A_{y}

A_{z}

\Delta y

\Delta z

2

3

1

A_{x}

A_{z}

\Delta x

\Delta z

3

2

1

A_{x}

A_{y}

\Delta x

\Delta y

,

which provides indices for the components of the magnetic field given a component of the electric field. The resulting variational state for time evolution of the electric field forwards by
\tau
is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sgn}\text{ }\frac{\tau^{2}}{\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}-\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }\bigg)\text{ }+\text{ }\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}=\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{x}}}}\text{ . }

We obtain the cost function by expanding the following norm,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{M}_{B_{\textbf{x}}}(\lambda,\lambda^{E_{\textbf{y}}}_{0},\lambda^{E_{\textbf{z}}}_{1},\lambda^{M_{B_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0})\text{ }=\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{x}}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}\bigg|\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow\text{ }

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg|-\tau\big(\frac{A_{y}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\frac{A_{z}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta z}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\big)\bigg|\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\Updownarrow\text{ }

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta y)^{2}}-\frac{2\tau^{2}}{\Delta y\Delta z}\bigg(\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }A_{y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }\bigg)\text{ }\bigg(\text{ }A_{z}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}-\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }\bigg)\text{ }\bigg)\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta z)^{2}}\text{ . }

Rearrangements of the superposition scaled in the second order difference
\Delta y\Delta z
corresponding to the first component of the magnetic field, is of the form,

\displaystyle\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}A^{\dagger}_{z}A_{y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}A_{y}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}-\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}A^{\dagger}_{z}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}+\bra{E_{\textbf{z}}}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ . }

From the state above, terms are rearranged further by introducing accompanying variational states for each participating component of the magnetic field. In particular, for variational parameters corresponding to the second and third components of the electric field, the superposition for the cost function takes the form,

.

and

.

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}^{M}_{B_{\textbf{x}}}(\lambda,\lambda^{E_{\textbf{y}}}_{0},\lambda^{E_{\textbf{z}}}_{1},\lambda^{M_{B_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0})}\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\big(\lambda^{M_{B_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta y)^{2}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta z)^{2}}-\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{2}}{\Delta y\Delta z}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta x}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{x}}}A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta x}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{y}}}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{x}}}A^{\dagger}_{y}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{x}}}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{z}}}A^{\dagger}_{z}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{z}}}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{y}}}A^{\dagger}_{y}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{y}}}\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}\text{ }}\text{ }A_{z}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}\text{ }}\text{ }\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{y}\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta y\text{ }(\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{z}\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta y\text{ }(\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{y}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }

where the variational states for the electric field, in addition to the second and third component of the electric field in the above state are,

\displaystyle\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\equiv\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{y}}}}-\text{ }\frac{\tau^{2}}{\Delta y\Delta z\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\Delta y\bigg(A_{z}\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{z}}}\bigg)-\Delta z\bigg(A_{y}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\bigg)\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }

\displaystyle\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\equiv\ket{\widetilde{E_{\textbf{z}}}}+\text{ }\frac{\tau^{2}}{\Delta x\Delta y\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\Delta x\bigg(A_{z}\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{y}}}\bigg)-\Delta y\bigg(A_{y}\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}-\ket{B_{\textbf{x}}}\bigg)\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }

from products of variational parameters. In addition to the cost function encoding the spatial distribution of magnitudes of electric and magnetic fields, in ZGR-QFT ansatzae for quantum readout of solutions we enforce additional initial conditions on the solution state with the other two remaining Maxwell equations, in which, for
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle\text{ }\nabla\text{ }\cdot\text{ }E\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\frac{\rho}{\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\text{ , }

\displaystyle\text{ }\nabla\text{ }\cdot\text{ }B\text{ }=\text{ }0\text{ }\text{ . }\text{ }

Similarly, to obtain a cost function for the remaining Maxwell equation, from
\mathcal{C}^{M}_{B_{\textbf{x}}}(\lambda,\lambda^{E_{\textbf{y}}}_{0},\lambda^{E_{\textbf{z}}}_{1},\lambda^{M_{B_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0})
one reads,

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{C}^{M}_{E_{\textbf{x}}}(\lambda,\lambda^{B_{\textbf{y}}}_{0},\lambda^{B_{\textbf{z}}}_{1},\lambda^{M_{E_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0})}\text{ }\equiv\text{ }\big(\lambda^{M_{E_{\textbf{x}}}}_{0}\big)^{2}\text{ }+\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta y)^{2}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{(\Delta z)^{2}}-\text{ }\mathrm{Re}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{2}}{\Delta y\Delta z}\text{ }\bra{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta x}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{x}}}A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta x}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{x}}}A^{\dagger}_{y}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{(\Delta y\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{B_{\textbf{x}}}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{z}}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{z}}}A^{\dagger}_{z}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }\Delta y}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{z}}}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}A^{\dagger}_{y}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}\text{ }}{\Delta x(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\bra{E_{\textbf{y}}}\ket{\widetilde{B_{\textbf{y}}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}\text{ }}\text{ }A_{z}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}\text{ }}\text{ }\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{y}\ket{E_{\textbf{x}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta x\Delta y\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta y\text{ }(\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{z}\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{\Delta y\text{ }(\Delta z)^{2}\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{E_{\textbf{z}}}\text{ }+\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }A_{y}\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }-\text{ }\frac{2\tau^{4}}{(\Delta y)^{2}\text{ }\Delta z\text{ }\mu_{0}\text{ }\epsilon_{0}}\text{ }\ket{E_{\textbf{y}}}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ , }

providing the second desired cost function.

4.6
Boussinesq type equation

Cost function derivation

The equation with smooth solutions
u
is,

\displaystyle u_{tt}-u_{xx}-2\alpha\big(uu_{x}\big)_{x}-\beta u_{xxtt}=0

\displaystyle\text{ }\Updownarrow\text{ }

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }-\big\{A^{\dagger}_{x}-2\textbf{1}+A_{x}\big\}\ket{u}-2\alpha\bigg\{\ket{u}\times\bigg\{\frac{A_{x}-\textbf{1}}{\Delta x}\bigg\}\ket{u}\bigg\}_{x}

\displaystyle-8A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

Rearrangements of the last term of the expansion imply that,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\bigg\{\text{ }\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}-2-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}+A_{x}\big)\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\big(2A^{\dagger}_{x}-4\big)\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}-2-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}+A_{x}\big)\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-\big(2A^{\dagger}_{x}-4\big)\ket{u(t+\tau)}\bigg\}

can be expressed as,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}+\cdots A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}

\displaystyle-2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }+4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

which can be further rearranged as,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-2\ket{u(t+\tau)}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }+4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau}

\displaystyle+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+\tau)}+4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\bigg\}

Further distributing terms,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\text{ }\big)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }+\text{ }\text{ }A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}

\displaystyle+4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\{A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}

\displaystyle+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}+4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}

\displaystyle+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}

\displaystyle-A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }+\text{ }\lambda^{2}\text{ }-A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+\tau)}

\displaystyle+A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ . }

Continuing, we group together several product states of variational states for the solution time evolved forwards one or two time steps,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg\{\text{ }\lambda^{\text{ }2}-A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }-A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}

\displaystyle+A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }+A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}4\ket{u(t+\tau)}-\text{ }\cdots

\displaystyle 2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}+4\lambda^{2}

\displaystyle+2\ket{u(t+\tau)}\text{ }\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}+2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-8\lambda^{2}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }2\ket{u(t+\tau)}-\big(\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\big)^{2}\text{ }\lambda^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}4\ket{u(t+\tau)}\bigg\}

which is equivalent to,

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{(\Delta x)^{2}}\text{ }\bigg\{\lambda^{2}-\text{ }\big(\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2-A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}4+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2\text{ }\big)\text{ }\ket{u(t+\tau)}

\displaystyle-\big(A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}-\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }-A^{\dagger}_{x}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }A^{\dagger}_{x}

\displaystyle+2\ket{u(t+\tau)}\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A_{x}\text{ }-2\ket{u(t+2\tau)}2A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }

\displaystyle-\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}A_{x}+\frac{(\Delta x)^{2}}{\beta}\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\text{ }2A^{\dagger}_{x}\text{ }\big)\text{ }\ket{u(t+2\tau)}\bigg\}\text{ }\text{ . }

The resulting cost function is factorized according to the time step that the variational state for the solution of the nonlinear problem is evolved forwards by,
